          - region "Article content" [ref=e99]:
            - generic [ref=e100]:
              - generic [ref=e101]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e102]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e103]:
                  - text: Hydrogen peroxide (H
                  - subscript [ref=e104]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e105]: "2"
                  - text: ) is the most abundant reactive oxygen species (ROS) within mammalian cells. At low concentrations, H
                  - subscript [ref=e106]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e107]: "2"
                  - text: serves as a versatile cell signaling molecule that mediates vital physiological functions. Yet at higher concentrations, H
                  - subscript [ref=e108]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e109]: "2"
                  - text: can be a toxic molecule by promoting pathological oxidative stress in cells and tissues. Within normal cells, H
                  - subscript [ref=e110]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e111]: "2"
                  - text: is differentially distributed in a variety of subcellular locales. Moreover, many redox-active enzymes and their substrates are themselves differentially distributed within cells. Numerous reports have described the biological and biochemical consequences of adding exogenous H
                  - subscript [ref=e112]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e113]: "2"
                  - text: "to cultured cells and tissues, but many of these observations are difficult to interpret: the effects of exogenous H"
                  - subscript [ref=e114]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e115]: "2"
                  - text: do not necessarily replicate the cellular responses to endogenous H
                  - subscript [ref=e116]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e117]: "2"
                  - text: . In recent years, chemogenetic approaches have been developed to dynamically regulate the abundance of H
                  - subscript [ref=e118]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e119]: "2"
                  - text: in specific subcellular locales. Chemogenetic approaches have been applied in multiple experimental systems, ranging from
                  - emphasis [ref=e120]: in vitro
                  - text: studies on the intracellular transport and metabolism of H
                  - subscript [ref=e121]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e122]: "2"
                  - text: ", all the way to"
                  - emphasis [ref=e123]: in vivo
                  - text: studies that generate oxidative stress in specific organs in living animals. These chemogenetic approaches have exploited a yeast-derived D-amino acid oxidase (DAAO) that synthesizes H
                  - subscript [ref=e124]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e125]: "2"
                  - text: only in the presence of its D-amino acid substrate. DAAO can be targeted to various subcellular locales, and can be dynamically activated by the addition or withdrawal of its D-amino acid substrate. In addition, recent advances in the development of highly sensitive genetically encoded H
                  - subscript [ref=e126]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e127]: "2"
                  - text: biosensors are providing a better understanding of both physiological and pathological oxidative pathways. This review highlights several applications of DAAO as a chemogenetic tool across a wide range of biological systems, from analyses of subcellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e128]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e129]: "2"
                  - text: metabolism in cells to the development of new disease models caused by oxidative stress
                  - emphasis [ref=e130]: in vivo.
                - paragraph [ref=e132]:
                  - strong [ref=e133]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: D-amino acid oxidase, Hydrogen peroxide, ROS signaling, Oxidative stress
              - generic [ref=e134]:
                - heading "Graphical Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e135]: Graphical Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e136]:
                  - img "graphic file with name nihms-1756274-f0001.jpg" [ref=e137]
              - generic [ref=e138]:
                - heading "1. The maintenance of the intracellular hydrogen peroxide balance" [level=2] [ref=e139]: 1. The maintenance of the intracellular hydrogen peroxide balance
                - paragraph [ref=e140]:
                  - text: Reactive oxygen species (ROS) is a term that has been used to collectively describe the various O
                  - subscript [ref=e141]: "2"
                  - text: "-derived molecules that can arise within cells. Numerous reviews have described the cellular metabolism of ROS in detail ["
                  - link "1" [ref=e142] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R1"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "22" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R22"
                  - text: "]; this review will focus primarily on the stable ROS hydrogen peroxide (H"
                  - subscript [ref=e144]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e145]: "2"
                  - text: ). It is very important to note that the various ROS are not created equal, either in terms of their chemical reactivities, cellular roles, or subcellular localizations. Some ROS–including hydroxyl (HO
                  - superscript [ref=e146]: •
                  - text: ), peroxyl (ROO
                  - superscript [ref=e147]: •
                  - text: ) or alkoxyl (RO
                  - superscript [ref=e148]: •
                  - text: ) radicals– which can form as by-products of cellular oxidative reactions– are so highly reactive that they are quite unlikely to subserve any physiological signaling role. Since no specific cellular defensive mechanisms exist for their inactivation, these highly reactive ROS can indiscriminately damage nearby biomolecules, leading to deleterious effects on cell function. The superoxide anion (O
                  - subscript [ref=e149]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e150]: •−
                  - text: ) is less reactive than the hydroxyl, peroxyl or alkoxyl radicals. O
                  - subscript [ref=e151]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e152]: •−
                  - text: can be generated in mitochondria by the electron transport chain, and can also be synthesized in various subcellular locales by a family of membrane-bound NADPH oxidases (NOX). Other intracellular enzymes can generate O
                  - subscript [ref=e153]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e154]: •−
                  - text: ", including xanthine oxidases, nitric oxide synthases, and cytochrome p450 monooxygenases. Under most cellular conditions, O"
                  - subscript [ref=e155]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e156]: •−
                  - text: is rapidly converted to hydrogen peroxide (H
                  - subscript [ref=e157]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e158]: "2"
                  - text: ) via mitochondrial or cytosolic superoxide dismutases. Under conditions of oxidative stress, O
                  - subscript [ref=e159]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e160]: •−
                  - text: can also react with iron and form the highly reactive hydroxyl radical (HO
                  - superscript [ref=e161]: •
                  - text: ) via the Fenton or the Haber-Weiss reactions [
                  - link "1" [ref=e162] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R1"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "2" [ref=e163] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R2"
                  - text: "]. Both because of its negative charge and its high reactivity, O"
                  - subscript [ref=e164]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e165]: •−
                  - text: is less likely to serve as a physiological cellular signaling molecule. The stable ROS H
                  - subscript [ref=e166]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e167]: "2"
                  - text: is recognized as the principal intracellular species responsible for redox signaling. H
                  - subscript [ref=e168]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e169]: "2"
                  - text: mediates a variety of crucial biological functions such as cell differentiation, proliferation, and migration in normal cells, yet at higher concentrations H
                  - subscript [ref=e170]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e171]: "2"
                  - text: also can cause pathological oxidative stress [
                  - link "13" [ref=e172] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R13"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "16" [ref=e173] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R16"
                  - text: "]. Physiological roles for the various free radical ROS have been more difficult to establish, and higher ROS concentrations have been implicated in multiple disease states including cardiovascular and neurodegenerative disorders, chronic inflammatory disease ["
                  - link "17" [ref=e174] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R17"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "18" [ref=e175] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R18"
                  - text: "], cancer, or the aging process itself ["
                  - link "19" [ref=e176] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R19"
                  - text: "]. The pathophysiological progression of such diseases can be prevented or accelerated by oxidative influences from the environmental factors of the so-called “exposome”, which integrates the effects on oxidative metabolism of lifestyle, nutrition ["
                  - link "20" [ref=e177] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R20"
                  - text: "], and physical activity ["
                  - link "21" [ref=e178] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R21"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e179]:
                  - text: This review will focus on the intracellular metabolism of H
                  - subscript [ref=e180]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e181]: "2"
                  - text: ", both as an endogenous biomolecule in mammalian cells and as a product of the chemogenetic recombinant enzyme D-amino acid oxidase (DAAO). H"
                  - subscript [ref=e182]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e183]: "2"
                  - text: is differentially distributed within cells, with highest H
                  - subscript [ref=e184]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e185]: "2"
                  - text: concentrations found in the peroxisomes and the endoplasmic reticulum (ER). Peroxisomes may serve as sinks for H
                  - subscript [ref=e186]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e187]: "2"
                  - text: "["
                  - link "15" [ref=e188] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R15"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "22" [ref=e189] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R22"
                  - text: "] while the high H"
                  - subscript [ref=e190]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e191]: "2"
                  - text: levels found in the ER might be required for redox-modulated protein folding and bridging disulfide bonds among cysteine residues [
                  - link "4" [ref=e192] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R4"
                  - text: "]. In most cells, the overall intracellular H"
                  - subscript [ref=e193]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e194]: "2"
                  - text: concentration is maintained in the low nM range by the balance of oxidative and reductive enzyme pathways [
                  - link "3" [ref=e195] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R3"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "5" [ref=e196] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R5"
                  - text: "]. In order to maintain this balance of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e197]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e198]: "2"
                  - text: ", different intracellular organelles contain a distinct combination of redox-active enzymes including catalases, peroxiredoxins, thioredoxin reductases, glutathione peroxidases and reductases as well as the small redox protein thioredoxin and the antioxidative tripeptide glutathione ["
                  - link "21" [ref=e199] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R21"
                  - text: "]. On the one hand, this differential distribution of redox enzymes and their substrates may allow the cell to selectively counteract oxidative stress in different organelles. On the other hand, the differential subcellular distribution of enzymes involved in H"
                  - subscript [ref=e200]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e201]: "2"
                  - text: metabolism facilitates dynamic physiological ROS signaling responses to discrete cell stimuli [
                  - link "16" [ref=e202] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R16"
                  - text: "]. In addition, the transport of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e203]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e204]: "2"
                  - text: between different subcellular organelles is itself dynamically regulated by peroxiporins, which belong to the family of aquaporin H
                  - subscript [ref=e205]: "2"
                  - text: O transporters. Peroxiporins are differentially expressed in tissues and within cells and facilitate the transfer of H
                  - subscript [ref=e206]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e207]: "2"
                  - text: through cellular membranes and participate in physiological H
                  - subscript [ref=e208]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e209]: "2"
                  - text: homeostasis as well as in oxidative stress (see
                  - link "section 6" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#S6"
                  - text: ) [
                  - link "6" [ref=e211] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R6"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "8" [ref=e212] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R8"
                  - text: "]. The wider view of redox signaling has been reviewed extensively, and will not be presented here in detail ["
                  - link "3" [ref=e213] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R3"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "9" [ref=e214] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R9"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "12" [ref=e215] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R12"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "14" [ref=e216] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R14"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "16" [ref=e217] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R16"
                  - text: "]. However, considering the complexity of intracellular H"
                  - subscript [ref=e218]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e219]: "2"
                  - text: generation, degradation, and inter-organelle fluxes, it is challenging to dissect the various oxidative pathways within different cells and tissues. Moreover, the detection limits of many biosensor systems have confounded efforts to sensitively and specifically quantitate intracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e220]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e221]: "2"
                  - text: levels in living cells, and the manifold roles of H
                  - subscript [ref=e222]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e223]: "2"
                  - text: in oxidative eustress and distress remain incompletely understood [
                  - link "15" [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R15"
                  - text: "]. This review discusses the development and cellular applications of a new and promising chemogenetic approach that utilizes a H"
                  - subscript [ref=e225]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e226]: "2"
                  - text: "-generating enzyme from yeast, D-amino acid oxidase (DAAO). Chemogenetic approaches using DAAO are successfully being used to dissect and decipher the complex pathways of ROS metabolism in health and disease."
              - generic [ref=e227]:
                - 'heading "2. DAAO: an old enzyme goes chemogenetic" [level=2] [ref=e228]': "2. DAAO: an old enzyme goes chemogenetic"
                - paragraph [ref=e229]:
                  - text: D-amino acid oxidases (DAAO) exist as distinct highly stereoselective enzyme isoforms within eukaryotic organisms. The DAAO enzyme and its cofactor flavin adenine dinucleotide (FAD) were first discovered by the metabolic pioneers Hans Adolf Krebs [
                  - link "23" [ref=e230] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R23"
                  - text: "] and Otto Warburg ["
                  - link "24" [ref=e231] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R24"
                  - text: "] in 1935 and 1938, respectively. Over the subsequent decades, the physiological functions of DAAO were identified and described in a broad variety of organisms, from yeast to humans, whereas plants mostly lack this enzyme ["
                  - link "25" [ref=e232] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R25"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "26" [ref=e233] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R26"
                  - text: "]. Different DAAO isoforms possess specific substrate-binding domains that catalyze the oxidation of D- but not L-amino acids with exquisite stereospecificity in the presence of the cofactor FAD. The oxidative deamination of the D-amino acid results in the generation of its corresponding α-keto acid, plus equimolar quantities of ammonia and H"
                  - subscript [ref=e234]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e235]: "2"
                  - text: (
                  - link "Figure 1" [ref=e236] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ). In mammalian cells, the DAAO co-products ammonia plus α-keto acid are usually present in much higher intracellular concentrations than H
                  - subscript [ref=e237]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e238]: "2"
                  - text: "["
                  - link "27" [ref=e239] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R27"
                  - text: "], so there is only a nominal change in intracellular ammonia and keto acid levels, whereas H"
                  - subscript [ref=e240]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e241]: "2"
                  - text: levels may increase markedly from their typically low basal levels following DAAO activation in cells [
                  - link "27" [ref=e242] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R27"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "28" [ref=e243] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R28"
                  - text: "]."
                - figure "This figure shows stereospecific catabolism of D-amino acids to its corresponding α-keto acid, ammonia and equimolar H2O2 by DAAO. The enzyme reduces its co-factor FAD to FADH2 and converts molecular oxygen to H2O2 (upper panel). Curves are modified from ref. 98, and show H2O2 increases in endothelial cells transiently transfected with the DAAO-HyPer and treated with D-alanine (right panel), but not L-alanine (left panel); the subsequent application of extracellular H2O2 leads to full oxidation of the HyPer biosensor." [ref=e244]:
                  - 'heading "Figure 1: Intracellular H2O2 generation by DAAO." [level=3] [ref=e245]':
                    - text: "Figure 1: Intracellular H"
                    - subscript [ref=e246]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e247]: "2"
                    - text: generation by DAAO.
                  - paragraph [ref=e248]:
                    - 'link "Figure 1: 🔍" [ref=e249] [cursor=pointer]':
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=8639655_nihms-1756274-f0002.jpg
                      - img "Figure 1:" [ref=e250]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e252] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F1/
                  - paragraph [ref=e254]:
                    - text: This figure shows stereospecific catabolism of D-amino acids to its corresponding α-keto acid, ammonia and equimolar H
                    - subscript [ref=e255]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e256]: "2"
                    - text: by DAAO. The enzyme reduces its co-factor FAD to FADH
                    - subscript [ref=e257]: "2"
                    - text: and converts molecular oxygen to H
                    - subscript [ref=e258]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e259]: "2"
                    - text: (upper panel). Curves are modified from ref.
                    - link "98" [ref=e260] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#R98"
                    - text: ", and show H"
                    - subscript [ref=e261]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e262]: "2"
                    - text: increases in endothelial cells transiently transfected with the DAAO-HyPer and treated with D-alanine (right panel), but not L-alanine (left panel); the subsequent application of extracellular H
                    - subscript [ref=e263]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e264]: "2"
                    - text: leads to full oxidation of the HyPer biosensor.
                - paragraph [ref=e265]:
                  - text: Much of what has been learned about DAAO catalysis derives from the work of Pollegioni and colleagues, who have characterized in detail the specificity of D-amino acids for DAAO from various species, especially those in humans and of the yeast
                  - emphasis [ref=e266]: Rhodotorula gracilis
                  - text: "["
                  - link "25" [ref=e267] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R25"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "29" [ref=e268] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R29"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "34" [ref=e269] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R34"
                  - text: "]. In humans, DAAO and D-amino acids are primarily present in selected regions of the brain and in some nerves, with a much more limited distribution in other tissues. However, all mammalian DAAO isoforms appear to have relatively low enzyme activity, and are predominantly localized in peroxisomes due to the presence of a peroxisomal targeting sequence; the generated H"
                  - subscript [ref=e270]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e271]: "2"
                  - text: gets rapidly and effectively eliminated by catalase, which is co-localized in the mammalian peroxisome [
                  - link "29" [ref=e272] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R29"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "33" [ref=e273] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R33"
                  - text: "]. Thus, under physiological conditions in mammalian cells, endogenous DAAO-generated H"
                  - subscript [ref=e274]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e275]: "2"
                  - text: accounts for minimal cytotoxicity even in the presence of D-amino acids. In specific regions of the mammalian brain, neuronal L-serine can be converted by the serine racemase to generate D-serine [
                  - link "35" [ref=e276] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R35"
                  - text: "]. D-serine can then activate synaptic N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors (NMDAR), which may have a neuroprotective role mediating physiological effects ["
                  - link "36" [ref=e277] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R36"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "38" [ref=e278] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R38"
                  - text: "]. In some subsets of neurons, it appears that the function of peroxisomal DAAO in neurons is to modulate the intracellular pool of D-amino acids, in particular D-serine. In selected regions of the brain, DAAO mutants lacking the enzyme’s peroxisomal targeting sequence can lead to a decrease in neuronal D-serine, which has been implicated in development of neuropathologic states such as schizophrenia ["
                  - link "39" [ref=e279] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R39"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e280]:
                  - text: In contrast to these very limited roles for DAAO in most mammalian cells, DAAO in yeast actually represents a key enzyme that plays a central role in energy metabolism. In yeast, D-amino acids are used as sources of carbon, nitrogen, and energy [
                  - link "40" [ref=e281] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R40"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "42" [ref=e282] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R42"
                  - text: "]. In contrast to the catalytic properties of mammalian DAAO, yeast DAAO exhibits a 400-fold higher flavin binding affinity ["
                  - link "43" [ref=e283] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R43"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "44" [ref=e284] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R44"
                  - text: "]. Analyses of the crystal structure of mammalian and yeast DAAO reveals that for both enzymes, the apoenzyme is a monomer that forms active dimers upon binding FAD, which then binds and oxidizes the substrate D-amino acid ["
                  - link "45" [ref=e285] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R45"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "48" [ref=e286] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R48"
                  - text: "]. FAD may be a rate limiting factor for the DAAO enzymatic reaction, especially when the recombinant enzyme is used in cultured cells. Indeed, it has been reported that the FAD concentration in many tissues can be orders of magnitude higher than the FAD levels found in cultured cells ["
                  - link "49" [ref=e287] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R49"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "50" [ref=e288] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R50"
                  - text: "]. Thus, the supplementation of > 2.5 μM FAD has been recommended for chemogenetic"
                  - emphasis [ref=e289]: in vitro
                  - text: approaches in cultured cells expressing recombinant yeast DAAO [
                  - link "51" [ref=e290] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R51"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, the binding capacity of D-amino acids for mammalian DAAO is lower than in yeast DAAO since the yeast enzyme does not possess an inhibitory active site “lid” that is present in mammalian DAAO variants. Thus, the rate of catalytic conversion of D-alanine to H"
                  - subscript [ref=e291]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e292]: "2"
                  - text: by yeast DAAO is more than 30-fold higher compared to mammalian DAAO [
                  - link "52" [ref=e293] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R52"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "53" [ref=e294] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R53"
                  - text: "]. The substrate selectivities of D-amino acids have been screened for several yeast DAAO isoforms, revealing a"
                  - emphasis [ref=e295]: K
                  - subscript [ref=e296]: m
                  - text: of 1 μM for D-alanine [
                  - link "54" [ref=e297] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R54"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "55" [ref=e298] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R55"
                  - text: "]; yeast DAAOs showed higher rates of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e299]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e300]: "2"
                  - text: production when D-alanine was the substrate, as compared to D-serine [
                  - link "56" [ref=e301] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R56"
                  - text: "]. Taken together the mechanistic and kinetic properties of yeast DAAO make it much more efficient at the generation of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e302]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e303]: "2"
                  - text: compared to mammalian DAAO; indeed yeast DAAO has been used as a biotechnological tool for the synthesis of cephalosporin antibiotics [
                  - link "57" [ref=e304] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R57"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "59" [ref=e305] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R59"
                  - text: "]."
                  - emphasis [ref=e306]: Unless otherwise noted in this review, the term DAAO refers to the yeast enzyme
                  - text: "["
                  - link "43" [ref=e307] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R43"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e308]:
                  - text: The term ‘chemogenetic’ has been used to describe model systems where recombinant proteins get activated by a highly specific and unique ligand or substrate [
                  - link "60" [ref=e309] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R60"
                  - text: "]. Chemogenetic approaches were used to develop"
                  - emphasis [ref=e310]: “designer” receptors activated solely by a synthetic ligand
                  - text: (RASSLs), which now are more commonly termed
                  - emphasis [ref=e311]: designer receptors exclusively activated by designer drugs
                  - text: (DREADDs) [
                  - link "61" [ref=e312] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R61"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "62" [ref=e313] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R62"
                  - text: "]. DREADDs have been principally used to study G protein-coupled receptors in the nervous system ["
                  - link "63" [ref=e314] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R63"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "64" [ref=e315] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R64"
                  - text: "] and enable an “on/off” receptor activation upon application of specific chemically engineered molecules or ligands. Chemogenetic approaches have also been developed using an enzyme’s unique substrate instead of a receptor’s unique ligand. DAAO represents a chemogenetic enzyme that generates H"
                  - subscript [ref=e316]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e317]: "2"
                  - text: only in the presence of its substrate D-amino acids. Beside DAAO, also other enzymatic models for intracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e318]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e319]: "2"
                  - text: production have been developed and utilized in the past such as uricase [
                  - link "65" [ref=e320] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R65"
                  - text: "], glucose oxidase ["
                  - link "66" [ref=e321] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R66"
                  - text: "] tyrosinase ["
                  - link "67" [ref=e322] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R67"
                  - text: "], glycolate oxidase ["
                  - link "68" [ref=e323] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R68"
                  - text: "], alcohol oxidase ["
                  - link "69" [ref=e324] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R69"
                  - text: "], L-amino acid oxidase ["
                  - link "70" [ref=e325] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R70"
                  - text: "], the NADPH dependent cytochrome P450 BM3 ["
                  - link "71" [ref=e326] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R71"
                  - text: "] as well as light-activated probes ["
                  - link "72" [ref=e327] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R72"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "73" [ref=e328] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R73"
                  - text: "]. But in contrast to the chemogenetic approach using DAAO and its unique D-amino acid substrate, these other enzymes can act on endogenous substrates, making it difficult to dynamically regulate intracellular H"
                  - subscript [ref=e329]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e330]: "2"
                  - text: generation due to presence of endogenous substrates. Therefore, researchers developed a chemogenetic approach based on DAAO to specifically and dynamically modulate intracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e331]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e332]: "2"
                  - text: mediated pathways.
                - paragraph [ref=e333]:
                  - text: One of several biological applications for DAAO is in the calibration and validation of H
                  - subscript [ref=e334]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e335]: "2"
                  - text: detection systems as well as for studying intra-organelle diffusion of H
                  - subscript [ref=e336]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e337]: "2"
                  - text: "["
                  - link "74" [ref=e338] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R74"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "77" [ref=e339] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R77"
                  - text: "]. Although several chemical probes, including the redox-sensitive dye dichlorofluorescine (DCF) ["
                  - link "78" [ref=e340] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R78"
                  - text: "], have been used to measure H"
                  - subscript [ref=e341]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e342]: "2"
                  - text: "["
                  - link "79" [ref=e343] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R79"
                  - text: "], these dyes lack specificity for H"
                  - subscript [ref=e344]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e345]: "2"
                  - text: as they can easily be oxidized by other cellular oxidants [
                  - link "80" [ref=e346] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R80"
                  - text: "] or may even generate ROS such as O"
                  - subscript [ref=e347]: "2"
                  - superscript [ref=e348]: •−
                  - text: "["
                  - link "81" [ref=e349] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R81"
                  - text: "]. In contrast, the development of genetically encoded fluorescent indicators has enabled more specific and sensitive detection of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e350]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e351]: "2"
                  - text: . Although some fluorophores can be pH-sensitive [
                  - link "82" [ref=e352] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R82"
                  - text: "], most genetically-encoded biosensors provide several advantages in live cell imaging. The commonly-used H"
                  - subscript [ref=e353]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e354]: "2"
                  - text: biosensors detect H
                  - subscript [ref=e355]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e356]: "2"
                  - text: as consequence of the H
                  - subscript [ref=e357]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e358]: "2"
                  - text: "-dependent oxidation of vicinal cysteine thiols leading to the formation of a disulfide bond, which results in a conformational change within the sensor protein and a change in its fluorescent properties. For instance, introducing cysteines at distinct positions of a green fluorescent protein (GFP) yielded the redox sensitive biosensor, roGFP ["
                  - link "83" [ref=e359] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R83"
                  - text: "]. The fluorescent biosensor termed “HyPer” is composed of a circularly permutated yellow fluorescent protein N- and C-terminally flanked by the split sensory elements of the H"
                  - subscript [ref=e360]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e361]: "2"
                  - text: dependent transcription factor OxyR from
                  - emphasis [ref=e362]: E. coli
                  - text: "["
                  - link "84" [ref=e363] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R84"
                  - text: "]. Ratiometric H"
                  - subscript [ref=e364]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e365]: "2"
                  - text: indicators based on Foerster resonance energy transfer (FRET) have also been developed in which a H
                  - subscript [ref=e366]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e367]: "2"
                  - text: sensitive peptide is inserted between two different fluorescent proteins [
                  - link "85" [ref=e368] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R85"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "86" [ref=e369] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R86"
                  - text: "]. More recently, several of these genetically-encoded biosensors have been modified, leading to enhanced H"
                  - subscript [ref=e370]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e371]: "2"
                  - text: sensitivity [
                  - link "87" [ref=e372] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R87"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "89" [ref=e373] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R89"
                  - text: "]. In particular, a novel FRET-based sensor has been developed based on peroxiredoxin-2 ["
                  - link "90" [ref=e374] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R90"
                  - text: "]; in addition the newest generation of Hyper sensors, HyPer7 uses the OxyR sequence from"
                  - emphasis [ref=e375]: Neisseria meningitidis
                  - text: "["
                  - link "88" [ref=e376] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R88"
                  - text: "], providing enhanced H"
                  - subscript [ref=e377]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e378]: "2"
                  - text: sensitivity in the low nanomolar range and also showing improved pH stability.
                - paragraph [ref=e379]:
                  - text: In contrast to most of the chemical dyes that detect intracellular biomolecules, genetically-encoded biosensors can be differentially targeted to different regions within cells, permitting the detection of H
                  - subscript [ref=e380]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e381]: "2"
                  - text: in specific subcellular organelles. DAAO can also be expressed as a fusion protein with HyPer, enabling the generation and detection of H
                  - subscript [ref=e382]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e383]: "2"
                  - text: by a single protein. The first study using HyPer fused with DAAO validated the kinetics of DAAO-mediated H
                  - subscript [ref=e384]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e385]: "2"
                  - text: generation, and demonstrated this production to be dependent on and titratable to the D-alanine concentration provided to the cell [
                  - link "91" [ref=e386] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R91"
                  - text: "]. By using a cardiomyocyte-specific promoter the same construct has been successfully applied"
                  - emphasis [ref=e387]: in vivo
                  - text: to study the effects of oxidative stress in rat hearts (see
                  - link "section 4" [ref=e388] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#S4"
                  - text: ) [
                  - link "92" [ref=e389] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: "]. In separate studies, DAAO was fused with an ultrasensitive FRET based H"
                  - subscript [ref=e390]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e391]: "2"
                  - text: probe [
                  - link "90" [ref=e392] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R90"
                  - text: "] or with a genetically-encoded NADPH indicator to study the effect of oxidative stress on the mitochondrial NADPH pool ["
                  - link "93" [ref=e393] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R93"
                  - text: "]. The development of novel genetically-encoded H"
                  - subscript [ref=e394]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e395]: "2"
                  - text: biosensors also revealed the existence of a large H
                  - subscript [ref=e396]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e397]: "2"
                  - text: gradient across the plasma membrane, showing that the ratio between extracellular and intracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e398]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e399]: "2"
                  - text: levels was ~400 to 1 [
                  - link "88" [ref=e400] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R88"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "94" [ref=e401] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R94"
                  - text: "]. Indeed, plasma levels of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e402]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e403]: "2"
                  - text: were also found in the micromolar range– orders of magnitude higher than those previously estimated to be present inside cells [
                  - link "9" [ref=e404] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R9"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "95" [ref=e405] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R95"
                  - text: "]. Since conventional experimental approaches using extracellular administration of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e406]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e407]: "2"
                  - text: need to overcome cellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e408]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e409]: "2"
                  - text: gradient to study oxidative effects within cells, the high concentrations of extracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e410]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e411]: "2"
                  - text: can result in spurious cellular responses, and should be interpreted with caution [
                  - link "96" [ref=e412] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R96"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "97" [ref=e413] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R97"
                  - text: "]. Instead, the applications of the DAAO system provide a more precise method to mimic endogenous H"
                  - subscript [ref=e414]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e415]: "2"
                  - text: mediated pathways in oxidative eustress or distress deriving from distinct intracellular sources [
                  - link "98" [ref=e416] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R98"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "99" [ref=e417] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R99"
                  - text: "] as summarized below and as shown in"
                  - link "Figure 2" [ref=e418] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F2"
                  - text: .
                - figure "Scheme shows physiological H2O2 signaling in the heart, vasculature, nerves, β-cells and other cells and tissues (left side) or conditions of oxidative stress resulting in cardiovascular disease, neurodegeneration, diabetes and cancer induced by pathologically increased H2O2 (right side). Depicted models were modified from Servier Medical Art ( External, opens in a new tab. https://smart.servier.com/image-set-download/), licenced under a Creative Commons Attribution 3.0 Unported License by Servier." [ref=e419]:
                  - 'heading "Figure 2: DAAO applications for studying H2O2-mediated pathways in various cells and tissues:" [level=3] [ref=e420]':
                    - text: "Figure 2: DAAO applications for studying H"
                    - subscript [ref=e421]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e422]: "2"
                    - text: "-mediated pathways in various cells and tissues:"
                  - paragraph [ref=e423]:
                    - 'link "Figure 2: 🔍" [ref=e424] [cursor=pointer]':
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=8639655_nihms-1756274-f0003.jpg
                      - img "Figure 2:" [ref=e425]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e427] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F2/
                  - paragraph [ref=e429]:
                    - text: Scheme shows physiological H
                    - subscript [ref=e430]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e431]: "2"
                    - text: signaling in the heart, vasculature, nerves, β-cells and other cells and tissues (left side) or conditions of oxidative stress resulting in cardiovascular disease, neurodegeneration, diabetes and cancer induced by pathologically increased H
                    - subscript [ref=e432]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e433]: "2"
                    - text: (right side). Depicted models were modified from Servier Medical Art (
                    - link "External, opens in a new tab. https://smart.servier.com/image-set-download/" [ref=e434] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://smart.servier.com/image-set-download/
                    - text: ), licenced under a Creative Commons Attribution 3.0 Unported License by Servier.
              - generic [ref=e435]:
                - heading "3. Chemogenetic H2O2-generating approaches in vascular cells" [level=2] [ref=e436]:
                  - text: 3. Chemogenetic H
                  - subscript [ref=e437]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e438]: "2"
                  - text: "-generating approaches in vascular cells"
                - paragraph [ref=e439]:
                  - text: The vascular system dynamically regulates blood flow according to the diverse metabolic needs of different tissues [
                  - link "100" [ref=e440] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R100"
                  - text: "]. The endothelial layer of the vascular system is critical in regulating this homeostatic system ["
                  - link "101" [ref=e441] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R101"
                  - text: "]. Oxidative stress is associated with endothelial dysfunction and with pathophysiological conditions such as hypertension ["
                  - link "102" [ref=e442] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R102"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "103" [ref=e443] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R103"
                  - text: "], vascular inflammation ["
                  - link "104" [ref=e444] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R104"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "105" [ref=e445] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R105"
                  - text: "], diabetes ["
                  - link "106" [ref=e446] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R106"
                  - text: "] and adverse vascular remodeling ["
                  - link "107" [ref=e447] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R107"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "110" [ref=e448] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R110"
                  - text: "]. Yet H"
                  - subscript [ref=e449]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e450]: "2"
                  - text: also has an essential role as a signaling molecule in the vascular endothelial cell to maintain this vascular homeostasis through involvement in post-translational modifications such as phosphorylation [
                  - link "106" [ref=e451] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R106"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "111" [ref=e452] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R111"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "114" [ref=e453] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R114"
                  - text: "], S-glutathionylation ["
                  - link "115" [ref=e454] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R115"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "118" [ref=e455] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R118"
                  - text: "], sulfenylation (RSOH) ["
                  - link "119" [ref=e456] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R119"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "120" [ref=e457] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R120"
                  - text: "] and other cysteine modification (cysteinylation and disulfide bond formation) ["
                  - link "121" [ref=e458] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R121"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "122" [ref=e459] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R122"
                  - text: "]. It is critical to regulate the optimal amount of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e460]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e461]: "2"
                  - text: in the endothelial cells to mediate these diverse oxidant-dependent post-translational signaling responses. Perturbed H
                  - subscript [ref=e462]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e463]: "2"
                  - text: homeostasis leads to impaired cell function, increasing the risk of disease-associated pathologies [
                  - link "108" [ref=e464] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R108"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "123" [ref=e465] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R123"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "124" [ref=e466] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R124"
                  - text: "]. One important issue in understanding this homeostasis is determining the actual concentrations of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e467]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e468]: "2"
                  - text: at physiological levels in different compartments of the endothelial cell. The recently developed Hyper7 genetically-encoded biosensor [
                  - link "88" [ref=e469] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R88"
                  - text: "], is an ultrasensitive probe for the detection of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e470]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e471]: "2"
                  - text: ", and can be combined with DAAO to modulate H"
                  - subscript [ref=e472]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e473]: "2"
                  - text: levels in different organelles in vascular endothelial cells [
                  - link "98" [ref=e474] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R98"
                  - text: "]. Using this chemogenetic approach along with live-cell imaging, H"
                  - subscript [ref=e475]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e476]: "2"
                  - text: can both be endogenously produced and detected in an organelle-specific manner in the cytoplasm, caveolae, mitochondria, or nucleus. Saravi et al. have recently shown that compartmentalized endogenous generation of H
                  - subscript [ref=e477]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e478]: "2"
                  - text: elicits differential signaling effects (through altered Akt, AMPK and eNOS phosphorylation) compared to traditional extracellular treatment of H
                  - subscript [ref=e479]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e480]: "2"
                  - text: in endothelial cells even though levels of H
                  - subscript [ref=e481]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e482]: "2"
                  - text: achieved by both methods were quantitatively similar in the cells [
                  - link "98" [ref=e483] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R98"
                  - text: "]. They also found that AMPK phosphorylation directly depends on nuclear H"
                  - subscript [ref=e484]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e485]: "2"
                  - text: . The inhibition of AMPK phosphorylation diminished nuclear H
                  - subscript [ref=e486]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e487]: "2"
                  - text: driven eNOS phosphorylation but had no effect on cytosol- or caveolae-generated H
                  - subscript [ref=e488]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e489]: "2"
                  - text: mediated phosphorylation response. This clearly suggests that compartmentalized H
                  - subscript [ref=e490]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e491]: "2"
                  - text: is critically essential for signaling events in endothelial cells.
                - paragraph [ref=e492]:
                  - text: Thus, spatiotemporal tracking of H
                  - subscript [ref=e493]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e494]: "2"
                  - text: levels in living cells is crucial for understanding the role of this redox molecule in vascular biology. A chemogenetic approach can be an invaluable tool for the study of vascular dysfunction caused by disturbance of H
                  - subscript [ref=e495]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e496]: "2"
                  - text: homeostasis. The opportunities that lie ahead with the newly developed HyPer7-DAAO sensors may be equally promising and should lead to a much better understanding of tissue-, cell- and subcellular-specific redox homeostasis.
              - generic [ref=e497]:
                - heading "4. A heart failure model based on DAAO induced oxidative stress" [level=2] [ref=e498]: 4. A heart failure model based on DAAO induced oxidative stress
                - paragraph [ref=e499]:
                  - text: Oxidative stress has been implicated in the pathogenesis of heart failure and in many cardiac disease states [
                  - link "125" [ref=e500] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R125"
                  - text: "]. Although these associations have been well described, it is still not clear if oxidative stress alone is a causal factor of heart failure. First insight into this question was recently provided by the application of chemogenetic approaches"
                  - emphasis [ref=e501]: in vivo.
                  - text: Specifically, a new model of heart failure was developed by using DAAO to generate the stable ROS H
                  - subscript [ref=e502]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e503]: "2"
                  - text: in cardiac myocytes in living rats [
                  - link "92" [ref=e504] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "126" [ref=e505] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R126"
                  - text: "]. The DAAO construct was packaged as a fusion protein with the HyPer biosensor in the cardiotropic adeno-associated virus isotype 9 (AAV9) under control of the cardiac-specific cTnT promoter ["
                  - link "92" [ref=e506] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: "]. The animals were then infected with the recombinant DAAO-AAV9 virus by tail vein injection, and robust expression in the cardiac myocytes was detected within 3–4 weeks. The expression was specific to the heart, with only minimal expression in skeletal muscle ["
                  - link "92" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: "]. Importantly, when D-alanine was added to isolated adult cardiac myocytes, there was robust generation of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e508]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e509]: "2"
                  - text: ", detected using the HyPer biosensor. Of note, the addition of L-alanine did not result in H"
                  - subscript [ref=e510]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e511]: "2"
                  - text: "generation: most mammalian cells use L-amino acids and the recombinant DAAO is inactive until D-amino acids are provided ["
                  - link "126" [ref=e512] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R126"
                  - text: "]."
                - paragraph [ref=e513]:
                  - text: To further investigate the effects of chronic oxidative stress in cardiac myocytes, animals were infected with the DAAO-AAV9 and were provided with D-alanine in their drinking water. This led to a striking dilated cardiomyopathy phenotype within 4 weeks of D-alanine treatment, characterized by significant reductions in left ventricular (LV) ejection fraction and fractional shortening along with significant increases in LV volume and heart mass [
                  - link "92" [ref=e514] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "126" [ref=e515] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R126"
                  - text: "]. The hemodynamic changes were accompanied by significant increases of the biomarkers atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP), brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) and cardiac troponin I (cTnI) both in the plasma and the cardiac transcriptome as has been similarly reported in humans with heart failure ["
                  - link "92" [ref=e516] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "126" [ref=e517] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R126"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "127" [ref=e518] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R127"
                  - text: "]. Despite the decline in cardiac function, there was no evidence of interstitial fibrosis by 4 weeks of D-alanine feeding. With longer provision of D-alanine, there was no further decline in cardiac function, but there was development of cardiac fibrosis at 8 weeks as seen by increased collagen deposition on histology and elevation of several transcriptional markers of fibrosis including mRNAs encoding selected collagen isoforms and transforming growth factor-β1. The robust heart failure phenotype was rapidly reversed when DAAO infected animals were treated with angiotensin-II receptor blocker valsartan alone or in combination with the neprilysin inhibitor sacubitril even in the presence of ongoing oxidative stress ["
                  - link "126" [ref=e519] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R126"
                  - text: "]. Interestingly, despite the recovery of cardiac function with drug treatment, the interstitial fibrosis persisted. These findings indicate that cardiac fibrosis and cardiac dysfunction should not be used interchangeably as the presence of one does not necessarily imply the presence of the other."
                - paragraph [ref=e520]: In conclusion, the DAAO model of chemogenetic heart failure induces a rapid, robust and tractable heart failure phenotype that is reversible and has many features in common with human heart failure. This chemogenetic approach adds new insights and establishes oxidative stress as a causal determinant of heart failure. This new model may now be utilized for the discovery and validation of new therapeutic targets for the prevention and treatment of heart failure.
              - generic [ref=e521]:
                - heading "5. Using DAAO in a neurobiological system" [level=2] [ref=e522]: 5. Using DAAO in a neurobiological system
                - paragraph [ref=e523]:
                  - text: In contrast to the deleterious effects of chemogenetic oxidative stress on cardiac myocyte function [
                  - link "92" [ref=e524] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: "], in other model systems the chemogenetic generation of lower H"
                  - subscript [ref=e525]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e526]: "2"
                  - text: concentrations can have cytoprotective effects. ROS imbalance is also a major cause of neurodegenerative disorders such as Alzheimer’s disease, Parkinson’s disease, Huntington’s disease, and amyotrophic lateral sclerosis [
                  - link "128" [ref=e527] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R128"
                  - text: "]. The molecular mechanisms, whereby alterations in redox balance lead to neurodegeneration, are incompletely understood, but several reports have implicated a neuroprotective role of the redox-modulated transcription factor nuclear factor erythroid 2-related factor 2 (Nrf2) ["
                  - link "129" [ref=e528] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R129"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "131" [ref=e529] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R131"
                  - text: "]. However, a study using cytosolic DAAO expression in astrocytes co-cultured with neurons successfully led to a redox-regulated neuroprotection that was independent of Nrf2 ["
                  - link "132" [ref=e530] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R132"
                  - text: "]. Astrocytes and neurons are tightly interconnected via gap junctions to ensure crucial cell communication between the two neuronal cell types ["
                  - link "133" [ref=e531] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R133"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "134" [ref=e532] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R134"
                  - text: "]. The exposure of DAAO-expressing astrocytes to high concentrations of D-alanine resulted in neurotoxicity, but the application of a lower D-alanine concentration enabled H"
                  - subscript [ref=e533]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e534]: "2"
                  - text: transfer to neurons that led to the inhibition of protein tyrosine phosphatase within the astrocytes. Microarray analyses of the axonal transcriptome revealed an up-regulation of Interleukin-1beta (IL-1β) and Caveolin-1, but no increased gene expression of Nrf2-regulated genes. The authors could mimic the neuroprotective H
                  - subscript [ref=e535]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e536]: "2"
                  - text: "-induced effect with PTP inhibitors, indicating a possible neuroprotective mechanism in response to IL-1β upregulation ["
                  - link "135" [ref=e537] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R135"
                  - text: "]. In contrast, a subsequent publication reported that extracellular H"
                  - subscript [ref=e538]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e539]: "2"
                  - text: promoted activation of Nrf2 in astrocytes under the same conditions [
                  - link "136" [ref=e540] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R136"
                  - text: "]. These disparate findings again point to the divergent responses that can be elicited depending on the cellular source of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e541]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e542]: "2"
                  - text: "["
                  - link "137" [ref=e543] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R137"
                  - text: "]. Despite the long-held view that neurons are particularly vulnerable to oxidative stress ["
                  - link "138" [ref=e544] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R138"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "139" [ref=e545] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R139"
                  - text: "], these studies show that H"
                  - subscript [ref=e546]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e547]: "2"
                  - text: can be involved in both physiological and pathological responses in neurons. Moreover, the work of Haskew-Layton et al. to date represents the only investigation in which DAAO-mediated H
                  - subscript [ref=e548]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e549]: "2"
                  - text: generation is used to elicit a physiological instead of a pathological response.
              - generic [ref=e550]:
                - heading "6. Visualizing inter-organelle H2O2 transfer with HyPer and DAAO in a diabetic model" [level=2] [ref=e551]:
                  - text: 6. Visualizing inter-organelle H
                  - subscript [ref=e552]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e553]: "2"
                  - text: transfer with HyPer and DAAO in a diabetic model
                - paragraph [ref=e554]:
                  - text: Pancreatic β-cells, which produce and secrete insulin, are the largest cell population of the islets of Langerhans and are particularly vulnerable to oxidative stress [
                  - link "140" [ref=e555] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R140"
                  - text: "]. This exceptional sensitivity is due to the fact that β-cells typically express low levels of antioxidant enzymes ["
                  - link "141" [ref=e556] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R141"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "142" [ref=e557] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R142"
                  - text: "]. Catalase is almost completely absent in the peroxisomes of β-cells, and glutathione peroxidases in the mitochondria and ER are only weakly expressed ["
                  - link "143" [ref=e558] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R143"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "144" [ref=e559] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R144"
                  - text: "]. On the other hand, β-cells generate considerable amounts of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e560]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e561]: "2"
                  - text: as a by-product of disulfide formation during the synthesis of insulin [
                  - link "145" [ref=e562] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R145"
                  - text: "]. Production of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e563]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e564]: "2"
                  - text: is further enhanced in pathologic states. During cytokine attack by immune cells in type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1D), large amounts of H
                  - subscript [ref=e565]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e566]: "2"
                  - text: are produced in the mitochondria [
                  - link "146" [ref=e567] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R146"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "148" [ref=e568] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R148"
                  - text: "]. In the pathogenesis of type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2D), H"
                  - subscript [ref=e569]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e570]: "2"
                  - text: is formed from the peroxisomal β-oxidation of long-chain free fatty acids [
                  - link "149" [ref=e571] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R149"
                  - text: "]. Thus, increased ROS production can lead to the development of diabetes, as H"
                  - subscript [ref=e572]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e573]: "2"
                  - text: cannot be sufficiently detoxified. It is therefore even more important that H
                  - subscript [ref=e574]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e575]: "2"
                  - text: can be transported to a site with a higher antioxidant capacity. Otherwise, toxic OH
                  - superscript [ref=e576]: •
                  - text: may arise in the Fenton reaction, which can lead to β-cell apoptosis [
                  - link "150" [ref=e577] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R150"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "151" [ref=e578] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R151"
                  - text: "]. Differentially-targeted DAAO constructs (targeted to mitochondria, peroxisomes, and/or ER)– in combination with targeted HyPer constructs– allow monitoring of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e579]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e580]: "2"
                  - text: transport across membranes. By expressing DAAO in the mitochondria and HyPer in the peroxisomes, it is possible to follow the path of H
                  - subscript [ref=e581]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e582]: "2"
                  - text: across two membranes from one organelle to another. This approach is used in research on peroxiporins, which are a class of aquaporins that transport not only water but also H
                  - subscript [ref=e583]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e584]: "2"
                  - text: . The various peroxiporin isoforms have different H
                  - subscript [ref=e585]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e586]: "2"
                  - text: transport capacities [
                  - link "76" [ref=e587] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R76"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "152" [ref=e588] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R152"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "157" [ref=e589] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R157"
                  - text: "]. Recombinant DAAO/HyPer approaches have been used to explore the localization and functionality of different peroxiporins by “knocking out” and/or overexpressing these peroxiporins and then using live cell imaging to quantitate H"
                  - subscript [ref=e590]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e591]: "2"
                  - text: transfer rates within the cell. For example, one study documented a critical role of the peroxiporin isoform AQP8 in modulating the intracellular distribution of H
                  - subscript [ref=e592]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e593]: "2"
                  - text: in insulin-producing β-cells, which are particularly susceptible to oxidative stress [
                  - link "75" [ref=e594] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R75"
                  - text: "], and indicating a potential role for AQP8 paticularly in T1D pathogenesis."
              - generic [ref=e595]:
                - heading "7. Targeting cancer cells with DAAO" [level=2] [ref=e596]: 7. Targeting cancer cells with DAAO
                - paragraph [ref=e597]:
                  - text: Excessive H
                  - subscript [ref=e598]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e599]: "2"
                  - text: can lead to pathological oxidative stress and cause cellular apoptosis [
                  - link "158" [ref=e600] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R158"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "159" [ref=e601] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R159"
                  - text: "]. One way to harness this oxidative damage for therapeutic purposes is to specifically target cancer cells for enhanced ROS production in order to induce cancer cell death. Many small molecule redox chemotherapeutics have been investigated for this purpose, but often have shown off-target effects ["
                  - link "160" [ref=e602] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R160"
                  - text: "]. The use of DAAO as a gene-directed enzyme prodrug represents a potentially effective approach since D-amino acids would not have a cytotoxic effect in cells that are not expressing DAAO. Moreover, some cancer cells appear to be characterized by increased ROS levels ["
                  - link "161" [ref=e603] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R161"
                  - text: "]. In a recent pioneering study, DAAO was stably expressed in rat 9L gliosarcoma cells, and the addition of D-alanine had a cytotoxic effect ["
                  - link "162" [ref=e604] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R162"
                  - text: "]. The main drawback of using this chemogenetic approach to kill cancer cells is the reliance on proper targeting of DAAO to tumors"
                  - emphasis [ref=e605]: in vivo
                  - text: since ROS are involved in almost every stage of cancer development [
                  - link "163" [ref=e606] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R163"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "165" [ref=e607] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R165"
                  - text: "]. To overcome this problem, the conjugation of macromolecules to DAAO has been used to achieve more specific targeting of DAAO to cancer cells; one combination that has been used to accomplish this was polyethylene glycol conjugated (“pegylated”) DAAO (PEG-DAAO). Tumor targeting by pegylation led to the accumulation of PEG-DAAO in solid tumors. Several studies in mice showed enrichment of PEG-DAAO in tumor cells, and the addition of D-proline resulted in marked inhibition of tumor growth as a consequence of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e608]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e609]: "2"
                  - text: generation– without affecting non-cancerous cells [
                  - link "166" [ref=e610] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R166"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, the tumor-killing effects of PEG-DAAO could be improved by the co-application of pegylated zinc protoporphyrin (PEG-ZnPP), a potent heme oxygenase-1 (HO-1) inhibitor ["
                  - link "167" [ref=e611] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R167"
                  - text: "]. Compared to normal cells, most cancer cells contain a more limited set of antioxidant enzymes ["
                  - link "166" [ref=e612] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R166"
                  - text: "]. Although these first applications of DAAO for cancer therapy appear promising, the method does have important limitations, e.g. the low O"
                  - subscript [ref=e613]: "2"
                  - text: levels in tumor cells led to the development of a DAAO variant that has a higher affinity for molecular oxygen. A mutated variant of a yeast DAAO was developed that showed a lower
                  - emphasis [ref=e614]: K
                  - subscript [ref=e615]: m
                  - text: for D-alanine even under low oxygen condition [
                  - link "168" [ref=e616] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R168"
                  - text: "]. Recently, DAAO-mediated chemotherapy has been further developed by coating magnetic nanoparticles with DAAO, enabling the use of an external magnetic field ["
