                  - link "169" [ref=e617] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R169"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "172" [ref=e618] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R172"
                  - text: "] to target the enzyme to tumors ["
                  - link "173" [ref=e619] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R173"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "174" [ref=e620] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R174"
                  - text: "]. Sikes and colleagues have established a model in HeLa cells that is based on mitochondria-targeted DAAO as an anti-cancer cell therapy ["
                  - link "74" [ref=e621] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R74"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "175" [ref=e622] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R175"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "178" [ref=e623] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R178"
                  - text: "]. The local generation of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e624]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e625]: "2"
                  - text: by targeting DAAO to mitochondria in HeLa cells showed more cytotoxicity than cytosolic DAAO [
                  - link "74" [ref=e626] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R74"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "77" [ref=e627] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R77"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "93" [ref=e628] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R93"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "99" [ref=e629] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R99"
                  - text: "]. Taken together, several approaches using DAAO have been developed to try and target redox-based chemotherapies to tumors, which may have the potential to serve as novel tools to combat cancer ["
                  - link "179" [ref=e630] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R179"
                  - text: "]."
              - generic [ref=e631]:
                - heading "8. Perspectives on the applications of chemogenetics in redox biology" [level=2] [ref=e632]: 8. Perspectives on the applications of chemogenetics in redox biology
                - paragraph [ref=e633]:
                  - text: Chemogenetic approaches using DAAO and HyPer represent an example of “synthetic biology”, in which engineering principles are applied to create novel biological systems that are not typically found in nature. Thus, a yeast enzyme (DAAO) and a bacterial transcription factor (OxyR, which is a key component of HyPer biosensors) have been cloned as a fusion construct and used both to generate (DAAO) and detect (HyPer) H
                  - subscript [ref=e634]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e635]: "2"
                  - text: in cultured mammalian cells or in animals. While these approaches are informative as long as proper controls are included (
                  - link "Figure 1" [ref=e636] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F1"
                  - text: ), there are potentially important differences between the biological effects of H
                  - subscript [ref=e637]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e638]: "2"
                  - text: generated by recombinant DAAO and the H
                  - subscript [ref=e639]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e640]: "2"
                  - text: that comes from endogenous enzymes and organelles. These differences are easier to control in the
                  - emphasis [ref=e641]: in vitro
                  - text: setting, in which real-time imaging of (using HyPer or other biosensors) can be used to dissect intracellular pathways of H
                  - subscript [ref=e642]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e643]: "2"
                  - text: diffusion and signaling following the addition (or removal) of D-amino acids. However, the results of these experiments can be influenced by the differential transport of D-amino acids in different organelles and in different cell types [
                  - link "180" [ref=e644] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R180"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "183" [ref=e645] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R183"
                  - text: "]. Moreover, the rate of reduction of oxidized HyPer might vary in different regions of the cell, and could thereby influence cellular responses. The H"
                  - subscript [ref=e646]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e647]: "2"
                  - text: generated by, for example, Nox4 in the endoplasmic reticulum, cannot be viewed as being equivalent to the H
                  - subscript [ref=e648]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e649]: "2"
                  - text: "molecules that are generated by ER-targeted DAAO: there will be differences both in local H"
                  - subscript [ref=e650]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e651]: "2"
                  - text: flux and in the protein microenvironment that exert influence the cellular response. It can be helpful to use a HyPer control construct (as control for the HyPer-DAAO fusion protein) to avoid potential confounding by HyPer serving as a sink for the H
                  - subscript [ref=e652]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e653]: "2"
                  - text: in the cell. In general, however, genetically-encoded biosensors are expressed at a much lower level than the analytes that they detect, so HyPer itself is unlikely to independently change basal intracellular H
                  - subscript [ref=e654]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e655]: "2"
                  - text: levels. It is also important to include negative controls that include the use of L-amino acids instead of D-amino acids. All of these considerations also apply in the
                  - emphasis [ref=e656]: in vivo
                  - text: setting, but once again, confounding can be minimized by the use of proper controls.
                  - emphasis [ref=e657]: In vivo,
                  - text: the consequences of D-amino acid addition and removal may transpire over a time period of days, and differential D-amino acid uptake between different cell types can also influence the response to chemogenetic H
                  - subscript [ref=e658]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e659]: "2"
                  - text: generation. It is important to note that the DAAO-HyPer fusion protein is able to induce chronic oxidative stress
                  - emphasis [ref=e660]: in vivo,
                  - text: indicating that DAAO generates much more H
                  - subscript [ref=e661]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e662]: "2"
                  - text: than HyPer is able to consume [
                  - link "92" [ref=e663] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: "]. D-amino acid feeding to animals expressing targeted DAAO in different cell types permits the study of oxidative stress in distinct tissues to be correlated with disease phenotype. These"
                  - emphasis [ref=e664]: in vivo
                  - text: approaches can provide important information of the differential roles of specific cells in ROS-dependent disease states. Despite these limitations, properly-designed and carefully-controlled chemogenetic approaches using DAAO can provide novel information on the effects of differentially-generated H
                  - subscript [ref=e665]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e666]: "2"
                  - text: both in cultured cells and
                  - emphasis [ref=e667]: in vivo.
                - paragraph [ref=e668]:
                  - text: Many decades passed from the discovery of mammalian DAAO in porcine liver to the exploitation of the yeast DAAO as a chemogenetic enzyme to produce H
                  - subscript [ref=e669]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e670]: "2"
                  - text: in mammalian cells.
                  - link "Figure 3" [ref=e671] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F3"
                  - text: shows important milestones on DAAO research from the discovery to its use as a chemogenetic tool [
                  - link "23" [ref=e672] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R23"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "24" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R24"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "43" [ref=e674] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R43"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "60" [ref=e675] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R60"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "92" [ref=e676] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R92"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "162" [ref=e677] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R162"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "184" [ref=e678] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R184"
                  - text: "-"
                  - link "190" [ref=e679] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R190"
                  - text: "]. At the time of its discovery, the presence of DAAO in mammalian cells seemed highly unusual since D-amino acids are found at very low levels in most mammalian tissues ["
                  - link "33" [ref=e680] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R33"
                  - text: "]. It has been speculated that mammalian DAAO functions in peroxisomes to catabolize the rare D-amino acids that can found in regions of the brain and in metabolically active tissues such as liver and kidney. While the yeast DAAO follows the same overall catalytic scheme as its mammalian homologues, the yeast enzyme subserves an entirely different physiological role: yeast DAAO metabolizes D-amino acids to generate energy and provide key metabolites for the cell ["
                  - link "41" [ref=e681] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R41"
                  - text: "]. The divergent cellular functions of mammalian and yeast DAAO are not only interesting from evolutionary point of view ["
                  - link "25" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R25"
                  - text: "], but the distinctively robust generation of H"
                  - subscript [ref=e683]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e684]: "2"
                  - text: by the yeast enzyme permits the use of yeast DAAO as a chemogenetic tool that permits the modeling of oxidative distress and oxidative eustress. These key features of yeast DAAO have led to exciting new discoveries on the physiological and pathophysiological roles of H
                  - subscript [ref=e685]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e686]: "2"
                  - text: in mammalian cells and tissues.
                - figure "The timeline shows historical milestones in research on DAAO and chemogenetics (left panel). The graph indicates the percentage of publications using DAAO as a chemogenetic tool for H2O2 generation in 5 year intervals (right panel)." [ref=e687]:
                  - 'heading "Figure 3: Chronological development of DAAO and chemogenetic research leading to chemogenetic DAAO applications." [level=3] [ref=e688]'
                  - paragraph [ref=e689]:
                    - 'link "Figure 3: 🔍" [ref=e690] [cursor=pointer]':
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=8639655_nihms-1756274-f0004.jpg
                      - img "Figure 3:" [ref=e691]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e693] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/F3/
                  - paragraph [ref=e695]:
                    - text: The timeline shows historical milestones in research on DAAO and chemogenetics (left panel). The graph indicates the percentage of publications using DAAO as a chemogenetic tool for H
                    - subscript [ref=e696]: "2"
                    - text: O
                    - subscript [ref=e697]: "2"
                    - text: generation in 5 year intervals (right panel).
              - generic [ref=e698]:
                - heading "9. Conclusions" [level=2] [ref=e699]: 9. Conclusions
                - paragraph [ref=e700]:
                  - text: H
                  - subscript [ref=e701]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e702]: "2"
                  - text: is a key signaling molecule involved in numerous physiological processes in diverse cell types. However, like other ROS, H
                  - subscript [ref=e703]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e704]: "2"
                  - text: at high concentrations is deleterious for cells. Over the last century, diseases characterized by excessive ROS accumulation– including diseases of the cardiovascular and nervous system as well as diabetes and cancer– have emerged as the leading causes of death [
                  - link "191" [ref=e705] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#R191"
                  - text: "]. Chemogenetic approaches using DAAO for H"
                  - subscript [ref=e706]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e707]: "2"
                  - text: generation allow disease states associated with oxidative stress to be effectively modeled, enabling the more precise understanding of the pathological processes caused by redox stress. To date, relatively few studies have used chemogenetic tools to modulate ROS
                  - emphasis [ref=e708]: in vivo,
                  - text: but the number of studies utilizing DAAO has been increasing steadily over the last decade (
                  - link "Figure 3" [ref=e709] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F3"
                  - text: ). The generation of transgenic animal models with tissue-specific expression of DAAO will enable future investigations of ROS-related pathological processes in diverse tissues and organ systems and lead to the identification of novel therapeutic targets.
              - generic [ref=e711]:
                - heading "Highlights." [level=2] [ref=e712]
                - list [ref=e713]:
                  - listitem [ref=e714]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e715]:
                      - text: Hydrogen peroxide (H
                      - subscript [ref=e716]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e717]: "2"
                      - text: ) is a key molecule that is involved in both oxidant signaling and oxidative stress.
                  - listitem [ref=e718]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e719]:
                      - text: D-amino acid oxidase (DAAO) serves as a powerful chemogenetic tool to generate intracellular H
                      - subscript [ref=e720]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e721]: "2"
                      - text: and to decipher oxidant-modulated pathways in normal cells and in disease states.
                  - listitem [ref=e722]:
                    - paragraph [ref=e723]:
                      - text: Chemogenetic applications are increasingly being used to study the roles of H
                      - subscript [ref=e724]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e725]: "2"
                      - text: in cardiovascular disease, neurodegeneration, diabetes and cancer.
              - generic [ref=e726]:
                - heading "Acknowledgements" [level=2] [ref=e727]: Acknowledgements
                - paragraph [ref=e728]:
                  - text: We regret that we have been unable to cite all the primary literature on redox signaling and oxidative stress due to length limitations for this article; this has focused in particular on the enzyme DAAO as a chemogenetic tool for H
                  - subscript [ref=e729]: "2"
                  - text: O
                  - subscript [ref=e730]: "2"
                  - text: generation from its discovery to cell applications. This work was supported by funds from the National Institutes of Health grants R21AG063073, R01HL152173, and R33HL157918, and from the Brigham Research Institute Fund to Sustain Research Excellence (to T.M.); CK is funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) by grant No. 231396381/GRK1947. M.W.-W. is funded by an Erwin Schroedinger Abroad Followship from the Austrian Science Fund J4466-B and F.S. is funded by grants T32HD-098061 and 5T32HL007609-34.
